People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

My wife says it’s just talk, but I know they’re warning me to be careful.\n\nDoctor: Do you actually hear the radio saying these messages or is it something you understand from what's being said?\n\nPatient: I don’t exactly hear it calling my name, Doctor. It’s more… from what they say, the announcer and the men at the domino table. I understand it’s meant for me, like a warning to be careful. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to understand what's going on around you or to follow conversations with others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not all the time. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, a car outside or the radio… even a bird… it pulls me off, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It jumps from the choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend… then back again. I can’t hold one thought long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when there's no one around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. In the afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name… or hum one of the choir songs. It sounds clear, like he’s right there on the porch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these messages and voices are all connected in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man… they say little things that feel meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I know it’s like a warning to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone or working on something, can you usually stay focused on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not always, no. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, some little thing—a car going by, a bird, the radio—pulls me off. Then I… I forget what I was doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man… they say little things that feel meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I know it’s like a warning to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone or working on something, can you usually stay focused on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not always, no. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, some little thing—a car going by, a bird, the radio—pulls me off. Then I… I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty doing daily tasks or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Some, yes. I still wash and eat and all that, but… I’ve missed choir practice a few times. The garden’s got spots I haven’t kept up with. And the domino games… I don’t go much now. I think I made folks uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I suppose. At dominoes I’ll tap the tiles… same way, over and over. And I’ve held my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others tell me to just play, so… I guess it looks strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often do things in a way that seems unusual or out of place to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. At dominoes, I’ve been tapping the tiles in the same pattern, over and over. And sometimes I hold my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others just tell me to play.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive actions, like tapping the tiles, do you feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t know that I’m trying to do anything. At dominoes I just start tapping them the same way, or put my hand up like that. Folks tell me to just play, so I guess it doesn’t make much sense.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts feel like they are connected and make sense to you most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think so… I don’t really pay it much mind. Sometimes I’m not sure how to explain what I’m thinking, but it seems alright to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the messages from the men at the domino table and the radio are part of a larger plan or scheme?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about a whole plan… but it feels connected, yes. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man, they say little things that are meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but I know they’re warning me to be careful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble putting your thoughts into words when trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It’ll jump from the choir to baseball, then repairs I need to do, then my friend, and back around… and I can’t hold onto one thing long enough to say it right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, a car outside or the radio… even a bird… it pulls me off, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It jumps from the choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend… then back again. I can’t hold one thought long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when there's no one around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. In the afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name… or hum one of the choir songs. It sounds clear, like he’s right there on the porch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these messages and voices are all connected in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man… they say little things that feel meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I know it’s like a warning to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone or working on something, can you usually stay focused on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not always, no. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, some little thing—a car going by, a bird, the radio—pulls me off. Then I… I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty doing daily tasks or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Some, yes. I still wash and eat and all that, but… I’ve missed choir practice a few times. The garden’s got spots I haven’t kept up with. And the domino games… I don’t go much now. I think I made folks uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I suppose. At dominoes I’ll tap the tiles… same way, over and over. And I’ve held my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others tell me to just play, so… I guess it looks strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often do things in a way that seems unusual or out of place to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. At dominoes, I’ve been tapping the tiles in the same pattern, over and over. And sometimes I hold my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others just tell me to play.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive actions, like tapping the tiles, do you feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t know that I’m trying to do anything. At dominoes I just start tapping them the same way, or put my hand up like that. Folks tell me to just play, so I guess it doesn’t make much sense.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts feel like they are connected and make sense to you most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think so… I don’t really pay it much mind. Sometimes I’m not sure how to explain what I’m thinking, but it seems alright to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the messages from the men at the domino table and the radio are part of a larger plan or scheme?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about a whole plan… but it feels connected, yes. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man, they say little things that are meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but I know they’re warning me to be careful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble putting your thoughts into words when trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It’ll jump from the choir to baseball, then repairs I need to do, then my friend, and back around… and I can’t hold onto one thing long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to think clearly or make decisions over time?\n\nPatient: I guess some, yes. It’s not so much decisions… it’s more my thinking gets moving too fast. It goes from choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend, then back again. Hard to hold one thought long enough to explain it right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F28\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because some things been bothering me. The men at the domino table, and sometimes even the radio man, they say little things… like messages meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but I know they’re warning me to be careful.\n\nDoctor: Do you actually hear the radio saying these messages or is it something you understand from what's being said?\n\nPatient: I don’t exactly hear it calling my name, Doctor. It’s more… from what they say, the announcer and the men at the domino table. I understand it’s meant for me, like a warning to be careful. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to understand what's going on around you or to follow conversations with others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not all the time. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, a car outside or the radio… even a bird… it pulls me off, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It jumps from the choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend… then back again. I can’t hold one thought long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when there's no one around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. In the afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name… or hum one of the choir songs. It sounds clear, like he’s right there on the porch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these messages and voices are all connected in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man… they say little things that feel meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I know it’s like a warning to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone or working on something, can you usually stay focused on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not always, no. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, some little thing—a car going by, a bird, the radio—pulls me off. Then I… I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty doing daily tasks or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Some, yes. I still wash and eat and all that, but… I’ve missed choir practice a few times. The garden’s got spots I haven’t kept up with. And the domino games… I don’t go much now. I think I made folks uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I suppose. At dominoes I’ll tap the tiles… same way, over and over. And I’ve held my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others tell me to just play, so… I guess it looks strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often do things in a way that seems unusual or out of place to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. At dominoes, I’ve been tapping the tiles in the same pattern, over and over. And sometimes I hold my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others just tell me to play.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive actions, like tapping the tiles, do you feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t know that I’m trying to do anything. At dominoes I just start tapping them the same way, or put my hand up like that. Folks tell me to just play, so I guess it doesn’t make much sense.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts feel like they are connected and make sense to you most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think so… I don’t really pay it much mind. Sometimes I’m not sure how to explain what I’m thinking, but it seems alright to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the messages from the men at the domino table and the radio are part of a larger plan or scheme?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about a whole plan… but it feels connected, yes. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man, they say little things that are meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but I know they’re warning me to be careful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble putting your thoughts into words when trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It’ll jump from the choir to baseball, then repairs I need to do, then my friend, and back around… and I can’t hold onto one thing long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to think clearly or make decisions over time?\n\nPatient: I guess some, yes. It’s not so much decisions… it’s more my thinking gets moving too fast. It goes from choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend, then back again. Hard to hold one thought long enough to explain it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. I mean, I forget little things sometimes, but I’m old. I haven’t noticed anything big with my memory.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and following conversations is something new that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s new exactly. It’s just… I notice it more now. If I’m reading the church paper or talking with my wife, some car outside, or a bird, or the radio will catch me, and then I lose what I was doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more… from what they say, the announcer and the men at the domino table. I understand it’s meant for me, like a warning to be careful. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to understand what's going on around you or to follow conversations with others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not all the time. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, a car outside or the radio… even a bird… it pulls me off, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It jumps from the choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend… then back again. I can’t hold one thought long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when there's no one around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. In the afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name… or hum one of the choir songs. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man… they say little things that feel meant for me. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, some little thing—a car going by, a bird, the radio—pulls me off. I still wash and eat and all that, but… I’ve missed choir practice a few times. The garden’s got spots I haven’t kept up with. And the domino games… I don’t go much now. I think I made folks uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I suppose. At dominoes I’ll tap the tiles… same way, over and over. And I’ve held my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. It’s not so much decisions… it’s more my thinking gets moving too fast. It goes from choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend, then back again. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Not all the time. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, a car outside or the radio… even a bird… it pulls me off, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. My mind gets going too fast. It jumps from the choir, to baseball, to things I need to fix, to my friend… then back again. I can’t hold one thought long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when there's no one around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. In the afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name… or hum one of the choir songs. It sounds clear, like he’s right there on the porch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these messages and voices are all connected in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man… they say little things that feel meant for me. My wife says it’s just talk, but… I know it’s like a warning to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking to someone or working on something, can you usually stay focused on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not always, no. If I’m reading the church paper, or talking with my wife, some little thing—a car going by, a bird, the radio—pulls me off. Then I… I forget what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty doing daily tasks or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Some, yes. I still wash and eat and all that, but… I’ve missed choir practice a few times. The garden’s got spots I haven’t kept up with. And the domino games… I don’t go much now. I think I made folks uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I suppose. At dominoes I’ll tap the tiles… same way, over and over. And I’ve held my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others tell me to just play, so… I guess it looks strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often do things in a way that seems unusual or out of place to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. At dominoes, I’ve been tapping the tiles in the same pattern, over and over. And sometimes I hold my hand up like I’m stopping traffic. The others just tell me to play.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive actions, like tapping the tiles, do you feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t know that I’m trying to do anything. At dominoes I just start tapping them the same way, or put my hand up like that. Folks tell me to just play, so I guess it doesn’t make much sense.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts feel like they are connected and make sense to you most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think so… I don’t really pay it much mind. Sometimes I’m not sure how to explain what I’m thinking, but it seems alright to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the messages from the men at the domino table and the radio are part of a larger plan or scheme?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about a whole plan… but it feels connected, yes. The men at the domino table, and sometimes the radio man, they say little things that are meant for me. My mind gets going too fast. It’ll jump from the choir to baseball, then repairs I need to do, then my friend, and back around… and I can’t hold onto one thing long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to think clearly or make decisions over time?\n\nPatient: I guess some, yes. Hard to hold one thought long enough to explain it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. I mean, I forget little things sometimes, but I’m old. I haven’t noticed anything big with my memory.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and following conversations is something new that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s new exactly. It’s just… I notice it more now. If I’m reading the church paper or talking with my wife, some car outside, or a bird, or the radio will catch me, and then I lose what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind works as clearly as it did when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Not as clear, no. It gets… busy. My mind jumps around too quick—choir, baseball, things I ought to fix, my friend—and then I can’t keep one thought long enough to say it right.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and feelings you've been describing stay with you all the time?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, no. It’s hard to say.
